  ⚠️ **Freezing and regenerating are both deliberate.** `--freeze` is a flag of its own, and `export_all.py` neither passes it nor runs the generator: the examples cannot change when `data/` does, so re-rendering 167 pages on every export would be churn for nothing. The consequence is stated rather than hidden — a committed page can lag the engine. What guarantees the example still *works* is the compile check, which runs against today's engine every time the suite does; the image guarantees only how it looked. The tab says **missing** or **stale** in the two cases where it has to compile, and compiles against the frozen data so its fallback cannot disagree with the pages beside it.

  👉 **Every import in a generated file is derived from where that file goes, never written down.** A neighbouring session asked whether the MWEs' depth was fixed by construction; going to look rather than answering from memory found that it was fixed by a *constant* — `"../../config.typ"` in the generator — while the Katalog's fallback derived its own. That is the dormant form of the fault that had already cost a debugging round in v0.64, where the scratch directory gained a per-process level and every import in it stopped resolving without a word. One derivation serves both now, checked at a depth the examples do not use, so arranging them into subfolders later cannot break 167 files in silence.

  **Rendered in German, once.** Three languages would be ~37 MB for a gain the documentation does not need: it is written in English, and the labels the data carries are German whatever the window says (§4bis). An example wanting another language calls `set-report-language` itself, which is better — the reader sees the command doing it.

  **And the level selector tells the truth.** The engineer found that 1 · 2 · 3 did nothing on `#show-polar-moment("SD_02-10mm", V: 12, level: 1)`. It could not: a per-call `level:` outranks the report's base level (§4). The buttons are now **shown and disabled with the reason beside them** — disabled and explained beats hidden, the override being what such an example demonstrates — and the same fact stops the generator rendering three identical pages, which took the set from 177 to 167.

  **`tests/test_commands.py`** grew to 16: every example compiles **from its frozen MWE** (so the suite no longer breaks when the project moves), every example has a committed page, none is stale, no page is orphaned, and `examples/lib/` matches the live engine.

  ⚠️ Found by the concurrency work of v0.64.2 and fixed here: `command_preview.store()` moved a freshly rendered page into the cache with `os.replace`, and two processes rendering the **same** example failed with `Access is denied` on Windows. Losing that race is not an error — the key is the content, so the page already there is ours. Reproduced with a cold cache and two suites at once, which failed before and passes after. The lesson is worth keeping: isolating a working path and tolerating a race on a shared destination are two different repairs, and this needed both.

  *Verification: `python tests/run_tests.py` — **144 checks pass**, 0 fail. Two full suites started together: 142 and 142, 0 failures, the check that could not pass before v0.64.2. `python calc/tools/build_examples.py` renders 167 pages. The report still builds.*

- **v0.64.2** (2026-09-14) — one temporary directory per process: two runs of the suite at once stop destroying each other's files (§11ter).

  Two sessions, working on this repository at the same time, each spent a day chasing a defect that was not in the engine. A full run gave **20, 21 and 29 failures**; the same tests run alone gave **0**. The failures moved between runs, which reads like a race in the code under test, and the message read like one too: `file not found (searched at ...\tests\.tmp\formula\probe.typ)`.

  It was the harness. `conftest.tmpdir(name)` **wipes and recreates** the directory it is handed, and the path carried nothing to tell one run from another — so run A removed run B's probe between B writing it and B compiling it.

  Two things made it expensive to name, and both are worth recording because they will recur:

  - **Every failure was in a suite that writes a `.typ`, and none in the nine that do not.** That categorical split is what finally separated "the harness" from "the code": a defect in `formula.typ` has no reason to spare `test_i18n`.
  - **A duration says nothing here, and was tried.** A run that aborts early is short, and a warm run is shorter still — the second effect being the larger. A green run at 25.2 s sat between two red ones at 39.6 and 40.6 s, and the same 44 tests took 24 s on a quiet machine against about 60 s an hour earlier. ⚠️ I reported a second, independent `test_formula` flake on that reasoning and **withdrew it**: there was no second defect. The peer session's counter-example is what caught it.

  `TMP` is `tests/.tmp/<pid>` now — which `command_preview` already did for its own directory, and which is why `test_commands` was the one suite that never collided. `cleanup()` removes this process's directory and takes the parent **only when it is empty**; not empty means a neighbour is still running, and refusing there is the right answer rather than a fault. It is registered with `atexit`, which is what covers pytest, since pytest never runs the runner. The removing now lives in `conftest` alone: `run_tests.py` carried a second copy of the `rglob`/`rmdir` loop, and a second copy is exactly what lets a repair be applied to one of them and not the other.

  ⚠️ **The repair put every probe one directory deeper, which could have broken it in the same breath.** Typst resolves a relative `#import` from the importing file, so a literal `"../.."` in a probe would have failed with the very message the repair exists to remove. It does not bite — every import a test writes is absolute (`"/commands.typ"`) or builds its own prefix at run time — and the last test below is what keeps saying so.

  Verified:

  | run | result |
  |---|---|
  | the suite alone | **142 passed**, 0 failed, 0 skipped, 170 s |
  | two suites at once, run A | **142 passed, 0 failed, 269.7 s** |
  | two suites at once, run B | **142 passed, 0 failed, 269.4 s** |
  | two suites at once, another session's pair | **142 passed, 0 failed** each, measured independently (188.2 s, 186.5 s) |
  | | (270 s each, against 170 s alone — the cost of sharing one machine, not of sharing files) |

  → **The neighbour rule was then observed rather than only asserted.** A second session had its own pair of runs live on this machine at that moment, so four suites were running at once. When these two finished they removed their own two directories, left that session's two untouched, and left `tests/.tmp` itself standing because it was not empty — which is exactly what `cleanup()` is written to refuse to do.

  New `tests/test_harness.py`, 6 checks — the first file here whose subject is the test folder rather than the engine, written because the defect it holds was mistaken for a defect in the engine by two sessions at once. It holds that `TMP` carries the pid, that a `tmpdir` starts clean, that `cleanup()` leaves a neighbour's directory and its file untouched, that it may be called twice over and survives an empty parent, that the runner keeps no second copy of the removing, and that no test writes a depth-dependent `#import`.

  ⚠️ **Two hazards this does not cover**, both in `test_export.py` and both outside `tests/.tmp` altogether: the ghost record planted in `data/positions/` (benign — both runs want it gone), and `test_the_export_is_reproducible`, which reruns the **real export into `data/`** while every other test reads `data/`. No per-process path can separate that one. It did not bite in the runs above; it is named here rather than left to be rediscovered.

- **v0.64.1** (2026-09-14) — *Ajuster* fits the model and not a four-metre square (§10quater, §11ter).

  The engineer clicked it on a bench frame 0.5 m across and got a view **12.4 m wide with the model 42 px of it**, a twenty-fifth of the drawing area, and put it down to the dimension he had just added.

  It was not the dimension. Measured with and without it, the fit came out identical to the centimetre — and identical for *every* model under four metres, which is the tell. `fit()` held every model to a view of at least `MIN_VIEW_M` = 4 m, and did it by growing the rect to a **square** of that side. `fitInView` keeps the aspect ratio, so a four-metre square letterboxed into a drawing area of 1070 × 400 px is fitted to its **height**, and the width blows out to 12.43 m.

  The floor was written for a model with nothing in it — its own comment says so, "a first point lands on a grid one can read rather than on a millimetre" — and a model that has been drawn does not need it: `MAX_ZOOM` is absolute (2000 px/m) and holds the top end on its own. So it applies where its comment says and nowhere else: when the model's own bbox spans nothing (no point, or one), centred on the figure so a first point put down at 5 m does not open off-screen.

  Verified, off-screen, at two shapes of drawing area:

  | model | before | after |
  |---|---|---|
  | empty, one point | 4 m grid | **unchanged** |
  | bench, 0.5 m | 12.43 × 4.53 m, 85 px/m, figure 5.6% of the width | 1.81 × 0.66 m, 583 px/m, figure 86% of the height |
  | frame, 6 m and 30 m | fitted to the figure | **unchanged, to the pixel** |

  New `tests/test_fit.py`, seven checks — the first test in the folder to touch a canvas at all, which is why neither the defect nor the repair would have been named by a run. It holds the repair (a small model fills the view, and half a metre is over 200 px), the floor where it still belongs (empty, one point, and a lone point at (5, 3) still in view), that everything four metres and over is fitted as it was, that the whole figure is always visible, that the zoom stays within `MIN_ZOOM`–`MAX_ZOOM`, and — from the other side — that a dimension **is** part of what is fitted: it makes the figure taller and the fit stands back to hold it, which under the floor it could not have shown.

- **v0.64** (2026-09-14) — the Katalog documents its own commands (§2, §10bis, §10ter, §10quater, §10sexies, §11, §11ter, §13, §14).

  **What nothing said.** `commands.typ` re-exports all of `lib/` with one wildcard — the v0.40 decision that lets a command added to the engine reach every section without an import to edit. The price had never been paid down: **202 top-level `#let` bindings** land in the scope of every `Sections/*.typ`, about 60 of them commands an engineer calls and ~142 the engine's own helpers, and nothing at the point of use tells them apart. An editor's autocomplete offers `substituted-rhs` and `tokenize` beside `show-calc`.

  Worse, the catalog that was supposed to answer this was two thirds blind. `build_reference.py` read `lib/calc-engine.typ` and no other file, and its `LET_FN_RE` required a `(` straight after the name. So **`var`, `eq`, `verify`, `val`, `unit`, `var-domain`, `var-unit`, the ten commands of `i18n.typ` and the four settings of the document** — `report-level`, `uniform-level`, `number-digits`, `report-language` — appeared in no catalog at all. That is the whole of v0.60 and v0.61, documented in `CLAUDE.md` and nowhere a reader would look while writing a section.

  **The commands tab.** The Typst-Katalog gains a last tab, *Commandes / Befehle / Commands* (§10sexies). A tree on the left: five categories — showing an exported calculation, calculating in the report, reading the data, formatting, the report and its language — and a sixth, **Internes**, folded, carrying the ~142 helpers with their signature and `file:line` under a line saying they are not commands of the report. A panel on the right: the summary, the remaining prose of the `///` block, an argument table with each argument's type, default and meaning, two calls to copy, and the worked examples.

  **An example is compiled, not described.** Each example sits behind an arrow — the engineer's own requirement, *afin de ne pas surcharger le listing* — and opening one runs Typst and shows the page it produced. The page is the report's own: `conf` from `config.typ`, then 16 cm wide, which is an A4 less the report's two 2.5 cm margins and therefore the measure its text is set to. The font, the line breaking and the right-hand citation column are the printed page's. A **level selector 1 · 2 · 3** appears on any command taking a `level:` and recompiles the same example at that level, which shows §3.7's three reports rather than describing them. Where the example writes its own `level:` — `#show-polar-moment("SD_02-10mm", V: 12, level: 1)` — the selector is **shown and disabled**, with the reason beside it: a per-call level outranks the report's base level (§4), so the buttons could only appear to do nothing. Disabled and explained beats hidden, the override being the very thing such an example demonstrates.

  **And documenting a command is now an obligation, not a courtesy.** §2 gains a **rule 14**: a command added to `lib/` is finished when it carries a `///` block with a summary, a bullet per argument, a `->` line and at least two compiling examples, and when it is placed in a category — or deliberately in *Internes*, which is the same decision made explicitly. The test enforces each of those separately, naming the command and its `file:line`, so the tab cannot quietly fall behind the engine.

  **One source of truth.** Every word the tab prints is read from the `///` blocks of `lib/`, so Tinymist's hover, `docs/typst-reference.xlsx` and the window read the same text and cannot say three things (§2 rule 1). What the tab adds is arrangement: the category, the two calls, the rendering. The examples are new — **126 fences across 60 commands**, each a fragment naming this project's real ids, set after the summary and before the `- param` lines, the shape `eq` already used.

  **Two calls, built from the signature.** *Appel complet* writes every argument at its own default and *appel minimal* the required ones alone. The strings are filled from the export, so a copied call names a sheet, a plan or a connection that exists — the v0.28 rule carried from the loads tab to every command. The **named** arguments are read off the parsed signature rather than written down, so one added to a command reaches both buttons with no Python edit; only `var`, `eq` and `val` need a hand, their positional tail being a `..rest` no signature can describe.

  **What the parser had to learn**, both gaps having silently dropped material: a `#let` whose name is not followed by a parenthesis, so a state is catalogued beside the commands that read it; and a fenced block, extracted as an example and kept **out** of the summary — harmless while only `calc-engine.typ` was read, since it carries no fence, and six polluted summaries the moment `formula.typ` was. `BULLET_RE` also learned to accept `..rest`, whose documentation was being read as prose.

  👉 **Four of the six fences that already existed did not compile**, and nothing could have noticed: the workbook never read `formula.typ`, and Tinymist does not compile a fence. Three were excerpts naming variables declared off-screen — which matters because **one example is one document and one run**: `#unit` sets a dimension's unit *to the end of the document* and `#var` writes into a document-wide registry, so batching a command's examples onto pages of one document would let the first silently change the second. The fourth named `SD001-V1`, a connection this project has not had for many versions.

  ⚠️ **A PNG and not an SVG, on a measurement rather than a memory.** One page of this report exported with `--format svg` carries 284 `<symbol>` definitions and 527 `xlink:href` uses: Typst writes every glyph as a `<use>` of a `<symbol>`, and QtSvg implements SVG Tiny 1.2, which has no `<symbol>` — the text would not appear at all. It emits no `clipPath`, mask, gradient or CSS, so nothing else was in question. Recorded in §13 so it is not proposed again on the grounds that SVG would scale better, which it would.

  **The rendering.** Through `QProcess`, as the PDF button does, so the window stays answerable; the level, the language and the decimals are written **into** the generated file rather than passed as `--input`, those two flags being read by `Statischer Rapport.typ` and not by the engine. The `.typ` goes to `000-Python/.preview/`, gitignored — it must be under the Typst root or `../../commands.typ` does not resolve, which is why `tests/conftest.py` puts its own `.tmp` there. The PNG goes to `%LOCALAPPDATA%\typst-katalog\examples`, outside the synced folder, keyed on the example's text, the level, the language and the newest timestamp across the three `lib/` files, `commands.typ`, `config.typ` and `data/` — so *Importer Excel* invalidates exactly the renders it should. A failure is reported in the **example's own line numbers**.

  **The workbook.** `Functions` now carries all 202 bindings, internals marked as such, each with its category, file, line, kind, `returns` and both calls; `Parameters` gains the category and the default read out of the signature; a new **`Examples`** sheet gives each example with a `compiled` column. 121 parameter rows where there were far fewer.

  **`tests/test_commands.py`** (new, 13 checks): the category list in both directions, a `///` block and at least two examples per public command, every documented argument a real one, the `typ-dry` exemption confined to the one command that needs it (`show-check`, nothing exporting a `checks[]` entry yet, §14), the preview document carrying the report's own setup, and **every example compiled** — 124 runs through a thread pool, ~28 s. It also holds, for the first time, §10bis's rule that no engine binding may shadow a Typst global: stated since v0.40, checked by nothing until the whole list became visible.

  ⚠️ The examples name this project's real ids (`"SL"`, `"Velo-Unterstand"`, `"SD_01-8mm"`), so the documentation is coupled to this project's export in a way it was not before. Deliberate, and recorded in §13: an example the window can compile and a reader can paste is worth more than one true of no project in particular, and the test says so out loud when the data moves.

  *Verification: `python tests/run_tests.py` — **128 checks pass**, 0 fail, including the nine full-report compilations and the 124 example compilations. `python calc/export_all.py` writes `data/` and the workbook unchanged in shape but for the three documentation sheets. The report still builds.*

- **v0.63** (2026-09-14) — one unit per dimension, a result that is never silently in the wrong one, and commands that say what they take (§10bis, §10quinquies, §11, §11ter, §13).

  **Three things the engineer met on one line.** v0.60 gave the report a unit algebra, v0.61 gave it one name per variable; neither settled the unit the **answer** comes out in. `pick-unit` chose it from a flat list, the first candidate whose value landed between 0.1 and 1000 winning. On an `#eq` multiplying two forces, with F in kN, the page read:

  ```
  M = F · F = 1 kN · 1 kN = 1000000 N^2
  ```

  A caret on the page, because a unit is printed as a *quoted word* inside math and `dims-text` — the fallback for a dimension the list does not name — wrote its exponents with `^`. A number nobody can read. And no way to say «les moments en Nmm».

  **One unit per dimension.** `default-units` is a table keyed by the dimension itself (`dims-text`), one entry each: mm, mm², mm³, mm⁴, s, kN, kNm, N/mm², kN/m, kg, °. No magnitude window. `#unit("Nmm")` replaces one entry from that line to the end of the document, the dimension being read off the unit given, so one call settles one kind of result and leaves the others alone.

  The window had to go rather than be worked around: 20 kNm is 2·10⁷ Nmm, outside 0.1–1000, so a unit chosen on purpose would have been ignored precisely where it was asked for. ⚠️ Its one merit goes with it — a stress printed in N/mm² and a snow load in kN/m² though the two share a dimension. **N/m² is one dimension**, the default serves the stress, and a line that wants the other writes `unit: "kN/m²"` or `#unit("kN/m²")`. The engineer chose that knowing both halves; a test holds it.

  **The table lives inside the registry's dictionary**, `d.units`, beside `d.domains` and `d.stack` — not tidiness but the constraint that put `var-domain`'s stack there: `#eq` computes and stores its answer inside `var-registry.update(...)`, and the function of a state's update cannot read another state. Two consequences: the shape of a stored variable did not change, so `show-polar-moment(N: "N_Ed")` and every existing test went on working untouched; and **`lib/calc-engine.typ` needed no edit at all**, the default table living in `formula.typ` behind `d.at("units", default: ...)`.

  **A declared unit the formula cannot reach no longer stops the compile.** It prints the answer in the unit the operands were themselves written in, with one small red sentence under it naming both — *Die verlangte Einheit N passt nicht zu den Einheiten der Operanden — das Ergebnis steht in kNm.*, through the glossary, in all three languages. Three reasons: the number printed is right either way; a report that will not build teaches nobody which of the two units is wrong; and the value stored stays physically correct, so the lines below go on computing, which a test asserts. A unit that cannot be *read* is a typo and still stops the compile.

  That needed the **natural unit** to exist: beside its dimensions, every quantity now carries `nat`, the units as the operands were written, multiplied, divided and raised through every operation. `natural-unit` writes it out concatenated the way `kNm` is written — and checks rather than trusts, keeping a spelling only if `parse-unit` returns the very factor and dimensions it was built from, and rewriting it with `·` where it does not. Mixed units compose literally: `kNm/mm`, the engineer's own choice over normalising them.

  **Two commands beside them.** `#var-unit("F", "N")` converts a variable already declared and prints it, the way `#var` prints — named after `#var` for that reason, the engineer having renamed it from `change-unit`. And `#var("F", var-of(...), "N")` now **converts**: until v0.63 it replaced the label and left the number alone, printing «7 N» for seven kilonewtons read out of the workbook.

  ⚠️ **A refusal written inside a `state.update` is silent.** `#var-unit` on a name the registry does not hold compiled cleanly at first: the function of an update runs only where something reads that state, and a document that never looks the registry up again never runs it. The check moved into the `context`, which reads the registry at every level. Found by a test that expected a refusal and got a PDF.

  **Big numbers.** Past 99 999 a number is printed `1.235 · 10⁶`, at the document's `number-digits`, with the exponent in superscript digits — never `10^6`, these strings being printed inside quoted words in math, which is what keeps a substituted operand one atom. Confined to `lib/formula.typ`: `fmt-decimals`, which `show-calc`, the tables and the figures print through, is untouched, and a test asserts it still prints `157000` whole.

  **Superscripts, and reading them back.** `dims-text` writes real superscripts, sorted so that one dimension has one spelling whichever order a formula built it in; `unit-exponents` gained all ten digits and reads a *run* of them; `sup()` carries both minus signs, `str(-5)` giving a typographic one. Every unit the engine composes can therefore be read back by `parse-unit` — which matters because it is stored in the registry and may come back as an operand.

  **And the commands say what they take.** The engineer hovered `#eq` while writing a call and got `eq(args: arguments) -> any`, with no mention of `unit:` — an argument documented in the header and listed in §10bis's table, invisible in the one place he was writing the call. `var`, `eq` and `val` swept everything into a single `..args`, so an editor could show nothing. Their named arguments are declared one by one now; only the positional tail stays variadic, which it must, Typst having no optional positionals — `#var("R_k", 7, "kN")` and `#var("q_k11", "q_(k,11)", 1.729, "kN/m²")` are the same command.

  An audit of all three `lib/*.typ` files followed, and found four kinds of defect, none of which breaks a build:

  - the three sink signatures above;
  - five public commands with no `///` header at all — `report-level`, `uniform-level`, `number-digits`, `position-ids`, `render-symbol`;
  - `render-symbol`'s header, which existed but was **detached** from its `#let` by one stray `//` line, so the editor showed nothing — since v0.49, on the one function whose whole job is explained in its header;
  - no return line anywhere but one, so every tooltip read `-> any`. 57 were added, `-> content` for a command that prints included: that is what says the result can go in a `grid` cell, which `Sections/005` relies on. The single pre-existing line, `-> int, none`, was respelt `int | none` so that all 58 read alike.

  Zero missing `- param` bullets, which is the one thing that was already right.

  ⚠️ One Typst trap on the way: the unknown-argument check had to become a `panic` inside an `if`, because **Typst builds an `assert`'s message before it tests the condition** — `repr(unknown.first())` on an empty array failed every correct call.

  `tests/test_doc_comments.py` (new) holds all of it: every declared parameter carries a bullet, no command is a sink, every block ends in exactly one `->` line, a union is written with `|`, and no `//` detaches a block. Each of the five was run against a planted defect and caught it.

  Verified: 115 checks pass (`python tests/run_tests.py`), nineteen of them new or rewritten; the report builds and its pages 12–13 are unchanged to the printed digit; and a control page renders the six cases — `1 · 10⁶ N²`, the red line, a converted unit, `#unit` set and set back, `7.46 · 10⁷ mm⁴`, and a variable carried from kN into N.

- **v0.62** (2026-09-14) — the Katalog imports and exports: two split buttons (§10quater, §13).

  **The window could import but not export.** It reran the pipeline and showed what came out; for the PDF the engineer left it for `build.ps1`, `make` or Tinymist. And its button said *Réexporter depuis Excel*, which names the operation as the pipeline sees it rather than as he performs it.

  **Two buttons now.** *Importer Excel* — the same `calc/export_all.py`, in its own process, unchanged down to its error dialog — and, beside it, *Exporter PDF*, which runs that export and then `typst compile`. The order and the rule behind it are `build.ps1`'s: the compilation runs only if the export exited 0, so Typst is never compiled against unvalidated data (§12), and a failed export stops the chain with the exporter's own message naming the cell.

  **Each has a little arrow.** *Importer Excel*'s menu holds one entry, its own: there is one way to import, the `make data` the Makefile names, and the arrow says so rather than hinting at others. *Exporter PDF*'s holds what `build.ps1` takes — *Tel qu'écrit (document)*, *Niveau de base* 1/2/3, *Niveau uniforme* 1/2/3, and *Langue du document* or de/fr/en.

  **They are checks and not commands.** Choosing one says what the next compilation will carry; the button is what compiles. The engineer chose that over a menu of one-shot entries, which cannot express `-Uniform 2 -Language fr` without carrying the cross product, and over a dialog, which puts a second window between a click and a PDF. The seven level entries share one exclusive `QActionGroup` across both submenus, so the interface cannot ask for `-Level` and `-Uniform` at once — the rule `build.ps1` can state only by throwing when it is handed both.

  ⚠️ **Three flags were offered and left out**: `-AllLevels`, the `report-N` variants and `-SkipData`. So the menu decides what the one PDF contains and never which files are written — every export from the window re-exports first and writes `Statischer Rapport.pdf` beside the workbooks, which is `.\build.ps1` with no argument, and §12's three routes go on naming one target.

  ⚠️ **The choices are not saved.** They live on the window, survive a language switch and a reload, and are gone at the next launch: a forced level remembered from another day is one nobody would look for. What makes the live state visible is the button's tooltip, which names what is checked and is empty while both are the document's own.

  👉 **The checks are kept** (§11ter), in `tests/test_pdf_button.py`: eight of them, each verified to fail when what it guards is broken. The one worth naming reads `build.ps1` and compares — the window spells the build command a second time, so `$Main`, `$Report` and `--input level=`/`uniform=`/`lang=` are asserted to be the script's own.

  👉 **Verified against the deliverable, offscreen.** The window was built with `QT_QPA_PLATFORM=offscreen` and its menu driven: the labels in all three languages, the menu's shape, `pdf_input_args()` for each combination, the exclusivity (*Niveau 3* unchecks *Uniforme 2*), and the choice surviving a rebuild. Then the compilation itself, with `REPORT_PDF` pointed at a scratch folder so the deliverable was not touched: as written (3 611 799 bytes), `--input uniform=1 --input lang=en` (3 488 606, shorter, as a level-1 report should be), a level the document refuses (the dialog, the status, and the previous PDF left untouched), `typst` taken off PATH (`FailedToStart`, the same dialog), and the chaining in both directions — a failed export compiles nothing, a successful one compiles exactly once.

- **v0.61** (2026-09-13) — one name per variable: `var`, `eq`, `verify` and a registry (§10quinquies, §10bis, §10ter, §10quater, §11, §13). And v0.60.1's two repairs, folded in.

  **The problem was that v0.60 was unreadable.** The engineer said so after using `eq` on a page of `Sections/005`, and he was right: a variable carried **three** names — the Typst binding `k-dens`, the `where:` key `k_dens`, the printed symbol `k_"dens"` — the three had to agree, nothing checked that they did, and the `where:` repeated the list of operands the formula already named. One line ran to four.

  **The registry.** `var-registry` (`lib/calc-engine.typ`) is one dictionary for the whole document; `#var("R_k", 7, "kN")` writes into it and prints, and a formula names its keys directly: `` #eq("R_d", `k_dens dot eta_w / (gamma_M \/ eta_M) dot R_k`) ``. There is no `where:` any more, and no `#let`.

  ⚠️ **`#var` cannot create a binding**, and no later version will change that: Typst gives a function no way to bind a name in its caller's scope — there are no macros, and `eval(scope:)` evaluates in a fresh scope. The engineer's `#var(N, "N", 0, kN)` is therefore `#var("N", 0, "kN")`, the name being the string. For the same reason `#eq(R-d, "R_d", k-dens dot eta-w / …)` is not possible: `dot` and `\/` are math and not code, and an argument is evaluated *before* the call, so `eq` would receive a number and not a structure to print.

  **How a command both reads and writes one state.** Each of `var` and `eq` is **two pieces of content**: a `var-registry.update(...)`, then a `context` that reads the registry back and prints. The arithmetic runs inside the update, which is a pure function of the dictionary and needs no state read; the `context` placed after it sees the result. Reading and writing one state from inside one `context` is the thing Typst cannot be asked to do, and this is the way round it. The domain stack lives *inside* the same dictionary for the same reason — an update function cannot read a second state.

  👉 **Step 0 was a go/no-go.** The whole design rests on a `state` updated inside a `grid` cell resolving in reading order; `Sections/005` has two columns that each declare `F`, `l` and `M`, so if it did not, the registry would collapse precisely there. Verified before anything else was written: the left cell sees `B` absent then 2, the right cell sees `B = 2`, and after the grid all three are there, with no layout-convergence warning. The fallback, had it failed, was to keep `#let` bindings and a lighter `where:`.

  **`var-domain`, the engineer's own idea, and better than what was proposed.** `var-scope[...]` would have pushed and popped, losing what a column wrote. `#var-domain("S10 - 0.55 kN Ende")[ … ]` names the set instead: a domain is a *key* in the registry and is never popped — only the current-domain marker is — so `#val("S10 - 0.55 kN Ende", "M")` still reads it further down the page. A name resolves in the innermost domain first and then in the default one, which is what lets `R_d`, declared at the level of a section, be named inside every column.

  ⚠️ A *domain* here is a set of names in the report, and has nothing to do with a calculation domain (§3.3), which is a kind of calculation whose instances are Excel sheets. The engineer's word was kept; the collision is stated wherever it could mislead.

  **`verify` replaces `test`**, and takes the relation as a whole: `` #verify(`F_Ed <= R_d`) ``. Either side may be a full expression, read by the same parser `eq` uses. **The printed sign follows the truth** — where `a <= b` does not hold, the line prints `a > b`, so what is written is a true statement and the ✘ is what says the relation the report wanted is not the one that holds. At level 3 the right-hand side is reversed, `F_Ed = 2.037 kN ⩽ 4.969 kN = R_d`, so the two numbers meet in the middle of the line, which is what the eye compares.

  ⚠️ It is **not** called `eval`, which the engineer proposed: `eval` is a Typst global, and `commands.typ` re-exports at the wildcard, so the name would be masked in every section (§10bis forbids exactly this). He chose `verify` from four.

  **An uncited row is no longer squeezed by a column it does not use** (v0.61.2, the engineer's second correction on first use). `annotated-rows` reserved its fixed 3.8 cm citation column -- 4.4 cm with the gutter -- for every row, cited or not. At the full text width a line wrapped four centimetres early; in a `grid` cell at half the width it broke after three centimetres and hyphenated, which is where the engineer saw it. Where no row of a set carries a citation the column is not laid out at all now. Only the two pages of `Sections/005` changed, checked page by page against the render from before.

  **A variable's description is read with it.** At level 3 it is set on the line, after the unit, behind an em dash -- `n_v = 5 --- Anzahl statischer Systeme pro Laufmeter` -- and not as a footnote at the bottom of the page (v0.61.1, the engineer's correction on first use). It is what the variable *is*, so it belongs beside it; `show-position` and `show-connector` already printed theirs that way.

  **Levels are the engine's, not a second scheme.** `level: auto` resolves through `resolve-level` like every other command: 1 prints nothing for a `var` and the answer alone for an `eq`, 2 adds the value and the formula in letters, 3 adds the description and the formula in numbers. A `level:` on a call forces that one line. The engineer chose this over a fixed default of 3, which would have printed every variable in a client report and broken §3.7's three reports.

  **The unit of a result need not be declared.** `preferred-units` names the unit an engineer writes for each dimension, and the choice reads the *value* as well: among the units whose dimensions match, the first that puts the number between 0.1 and 1000. That is what lets one report print a stress as 12 N/mm² and a snow load as 1.729 kN/m², which share a dimension and would otherwise have to share a unit. `M_Ed / a` now comes to `2.037 kN` where v0.60 said `2037.037 N`. ⚠️ Written with real superscripts, since the chosen unit is printed as a quoted word inside math and a caret would come out as a caret.

  **The Katalog copies the new call.** The *Charges* tab's var rows offer `#var("a_k", var-of("dead-load", "EL+AL-Vordach", "ak"))` beside the old `#let ak = var-of(...)`, built by `build_reference.call_var` — the one place a Typst call is spelled out (§10ter). Its key is the printed symbol where a formula can spell it and the export's own name otherwise; `#var` keeps the symbol the value brought with it, so a value out of Excel prints as `data/` spells it, `q_(k,11)` and all, and becomes citable in a hand-written formula with its provenance intact.

  **`show-polar-moment` takes a name.** `N: "N_Ed"` resolves in its own context, since a registry value cannot be read outside one and an argument is evaluated before the callee's context is entered.

  **Two repairs from v0.60.1, folded in here.** ⚠️ *tinymist died on every keystroke.* `lib/formula.typ`'s `tokenize` was one chain of nine `else if` inside a `while`, and tinymist's type checker — which runs for completion and inlay hints — recursed over it until it overflowed its stack (`has overflowed its stack`, exit 3221225725). The document compiled perfectly throughout and `tinymist lint` was silent, so nothing in the folder caught it; a bigger stack does not help (`RUST_MIN_STACK` up to 1 GB was tried), and no upstream issue exists. `read-token` now gives every case one line and reads what it needs in a function of its own. `tests/test_editor.py` is what keeps it fixed: it opens every `.typ` of the report in tinymist and asks it the two questions that crashed, and it was verified to fail against the old file. ⚠️ *A substituted operand was two atoms.* `M / a` substituted as `0.275 "kNm" / 135 "mm"` printed as 0.275 · (kNm/135) · mm — a formula that does not state what was computed, which is the one thing this file exists to prevent. A value with a unit is quoted whole now, the way `verify` writes its sides.

  **Verified.** 85 tests pass, `tests/test_formula.py` rewritten around the registry (33 of them) with the domains, the unknown name, the hyphen in a key, the preference table and `verify`'s refusals each fired on purpose. And the numbers of `Sections/005` are unchanged, which is the only proof that the new front door altered nothing: R_d = 2.655 kN and 4.969 kN, M = 0.275 kNm, F_Ed = 2.037 kN, η = 0.767 and 0.41.

- **v0.60** (2026-09-13) — `eq`, a calculation written by hand that the engine computes — units included (§10quinquies, §10bis, §11, §13, §14). And the workbook defect v0.59 found, repaired.

  **The problem, restated from the page it was found on.** `show-calc` derives its numbers line from its letters line, so the two cannot drift (§10bis) — and that guarantee stops at the edge of `data/`. A calculation written in `Sections/` had to be written twice, once to compute and once to display, with nothing comparing them, and three lines of `Sections/005` had come apart: `M = 1 kN · 0.5 m = 0.25 kNm` where the code said `F * l/2`; `F = 0.275 kNm · 10⁶ / 135 mm = 2.037 kN`, which as printed gives 2037; and a comparison written twice over, ending in a `= R_d` that means nothing.

  **`eq` takes the formula once**, in ordinary Typst math source, and displays it, substitutes it, and **evaluates it**. Every math command works — integrals with bounds, second derivatives, indexed sums, matrices, limits, the slanted division, `bold()`, `overline()`, all verified — because the formula *is* math source. What cannot be evaluated still prints, with `value:` giving the answer. It returns a var object, so the next line takes it in its own `where:` and `#test` compares it; `show-eq` is the same call with nothing returned.

  **The unit algebra**, which the engineer asked for by name, is the half that earns its keep. A unit is parsed into a scale factor and a map of base dimensions — `kN` is (1000, N:1), `N/mm²` is (1e6, N:1 m:-2), the same dimensions as `kN/m²` a thousand apart — and the operations propagate them: multiplication adds exponents, a sum requires them equal, `sqrt` halves them, and the answer is converted into the unit declared. So `M_"Ed" / a`, kNm over mm, comes to **kN**, and the `· 10⁶` and `· 1000` the report used to carry are not written any more. Reading a unit is a rule rather than a table of special cases: at each position the longest prefix-plus-base match wins, which tells `mm` from `m·m` and reads `kNm` as kN·m. **A literal may carry its own unit** (`350 "m"`, `0.4 "kN/m²"`), which SIA 261 5.2.6 needs — its 350 is a length and its 0.4 a pressure, and without them the sum is dimensionally false.

  ⚠️ **What it cannot catch, and is tested for not catching:** a magnitude error whose units line up. `1000` is dimensionless, so `M / a dot 1000` is valid and comes to 2037 kN. The algebra checks dimensions, never sizes; a test asserts that wrong answer on purpose, so that nobody later reads the feature as a guarantee it does not give. What guards against it is the substituted line, which prints the factor where a reader sees it.

  **Where it lives.** `lib/formula.typ`, a second library file: the unit algebra and a recursive-descent parser, which `calc-engine.typ` has no other use for. It imports the three display helpers it prints through from `calc-engine.typ`, which knows nothing of it, and `commands.typ` imports both with a wildcard — the arrangement §10bis reserved for exactly this.

  **The six questions the engineer answered**, each applied as decided: both key forms (an identifier requoted by §3.1's own rule, and a string used verbatim for `q_(k,11)`); the unit algebra; both `eq` and `show-eq`; named `unit:`/`style:` with the tuple `("R_d", "kN", "bold")` also accepted; `style` as a string, since `bold` does not exist in code mode; and `norm:`/`norm-ref:`, both optional, printed in the same right-hand column as every other citation.

  **`02_Belastungsannahmen.xlsm` repaired.** The dry Dachbegrünung had been obtained by typing 100 over the XLOOKUP in `I9` and 1 over the load formula in `K9`, while `E9` went on naming the wet variant at 160 kg/m². Repaired in the workbook's own idiom: rows 300–306 of `Material (hier ergänzen)` are formulas mirroring the "hier ergänzen" rows 4–12 into `Tabelle2`, so the dry material went into row 7, where the sheet invites it, and row 302 picks it up. ⚠️ Written by editing the XML inside the `.xlsm`, never with openpyxl, which cannot round-trip a workbook carrying VBA macros, 58 images, 3 charts, 15 ActiveX controls, 8 data validations and the five structured tables its XLOOKUPs point at; 229 of the 235 parts came out byte-identical, and `calcChain.xml` was dropped so Excel rebuilds it. The build-up sums to 1.812 kN/m² now — what the sheet itself shows — and the chosen 1.900 is a round-up again.

  **A translation that was never made** is caught now, in either glossary (`tests/test_i18n.py`). Calibrating it was the whole difficulty, and §14 had said so before it existed: a naive comparison reports 107 entries, of which nearly all are words that are the same by nature ("Norm", "Position", "horizontal") or format strings with no words at all. Narrowed to a string of more than one word and more than twelve characters once its holes are removed, identical in two languages — four hits on the current tree, all four genuinely the same, listed by hand. Verified to catch a French sentence planted in the German dict.

  **§14's attribution question is closed** without being implemented. Three shapes were weighed — nothing in the data, a `positions` field on the *calculation*, a `domains` block on the *position* — and the engineer chose the first and judged the second unlikely ever to be built: with as many loads, combinations and checks as come into play, stating in Excel what every calculation concerns would cost more to keep true than the completeness loop it buys. What a calculation concerns is a comment beside the call.

  Verified: **69 checks pass, none skipped, none failing** — 19 of them new, running Typst itself and reading the numbers back out of the compiled document. Among them: every unit this project writes, parsed to its factor and its dimensions; `s_k` and `q_k` of the real `SL` sheet computed by `eq` from that sheet's own coefficients and equal to the export's to 1e-9; a stress computed in `N/mm²` and in `kN/m²`, a thousand apart; `q_(k,11) · b_e` equal to what `statics.py` computes; and seven refusals fired on purpose. The report compiles in three languages and at three uniform levels, with no warning.

- **v0.59** (2026-09-13) — The review's fixes (§3.3ter, §3.3quater, §10quater, §11, §11ter, §13, §14). A full reading of the 21 677 hand-written lines, the ten schemas and the report they produce, and then what it turned up. The engineer went through the findings one by one and decided each.

  **What the reading found that was good, and that is not touched:** the export is reproducible to the byte, all ten artifacts are validated before anything is written, Python and Typst agree to the last printed digit on the one calculation written twice (`SD_02-10mm`: I_p = 20 800 mm², F₁ = 11.475 kN, fasteners 1 and 3), no German leaks into the French report out of the 184 glossary strings that could, and five public functions in all of `calc/` have no caller.

  **New: `tests/`** (§11ter). Every verification `CHANGELOG.md` has ever claimed — more than six hundred checks — had been written once, run once and thrown away, so none of them could be run again. They are kept now, in six files by concern, run with `python tests/run_tests.py`: a sixty-line standard-library runner, no framework, and `pytest` finds the same files unchanged where it is installed. A test builds its own workbook (`tests/fixtures/workbook.py`) rather than reading the engineer's. Among them the two the engineer asked for by name: **`test_arrows.py`**, which hands Python and Typst the same figure and the same forces and compares their SVG element by element over four sets of N, V and M — verified to catch a divergence, `ACTION_WIDTH` changed on one side alone failing with both stroke widths named — and **`test_constants.py`**, which compares the allowed-value lists `validation.py` restates against the modules they were copied from. 41 checks pass, 1 is skipped, 1 fails on purpose (below).

  **New: `requirements.txt`** (§11ter), the three packages pinned exactly, and `calc/tools/dependencies.py`, which the **Katalog** runs on every start: an offline comparison against that file, then a threaded request to PyPI whose answer arrives as a banner with an *Install* button. Installing also rewrites the pin. Ten strings in three languages; nothing installs without a yes.

  **The permanent loads gained two rules** (§3.3ter), and the first of them found a defect that had been invisible. `EL+AL-Dach ohne Wasser` sums its layers to 2.412 kN/m² while its chosen value says 1.900 — and the reason turned out to be sharper than the symptom: `I9` and `K9` had been **typed over** with 100 and 1, to get a dry Dachbegrünung, while `E9` went on naming the wet one at 160 kg/m². Python follows the name, the sheet showed the number, and nothing on the page gave it away. So: a chosen value may not sit below the layers it is chosen from (rounding allowed for), and a specific weight may not be typed over the sheet's own lookup — `_check_weight_formula` reads the cell's *formula*, the instrument `_check_mu_helper` already uses on the snow sheets. ⚠️ The export is stopped until the workbook is repaired; §13 records why that is a stop and not a warning.

  **Smaller, each in its section.** The export **sweeps** the record of a position Excel no longer has (`data/positions/D001.json` had outlived its row, and `show-position("D001")` went on printing it) — the balancing loop the connections, plans and models have always had. Every schema **pins its `schema_version`** with `const`, and the validator was taught `const`/`enum`, which it had been declaring in the schemas and walking past. `wind.py`'s check of the local forces **goes on to the next surface** instead of returning at the first it cannot read, and says how many of the eight it actually checked (§3.3quater). `config.typ` lost the orphaned body of the `verdict` command v0.58 replaced — a `let` and a stray brace that compiled only because Typst read them as text. `.gitignore` was uncommented and 27 `.pyc` files untracked.

  **Deleted: `domains/bending.py` and `domains/combinations.py`** (§13, §14). Uncalled since v0.11, 47 versions, and not in fact "unchanged and still correct": both carried norm values hardcoded (`f_m_k = 24`, `gamma_M = 1.25`, `k_mod = 0.9`, `gamma_G = 1.35`, `gamma_Q = 1.5`) where §11 says a norm value lives in `norms/` alone, and both had their labels in English where the glossary is keyed by German. The engineer chose deletion over repair: they are written again from the clause when a caller exists.

  **`CLAUDE.md` was split and trimmed.** This file is §15, moved out — 179 KB of the 532 KB the reference had reached — and reordered newest first, all 58 entries. The status line at the top of `CLAUDE.md` was 56 KB on one line, of which 53 KB were 35 blocks of "Unchanged from v0.xx" duplicating this file exactly; it is 2.4 KB now and points here. Its §11 tree also stopped announcing four files that have never existed (`deflection.py`, `fire.py`, `sia265_1_tables.py`, `sia265_1_tables.json`). **532 KB → 316 KB.**

  Verified: 41 of the 43 checks pass and one is skipped (the export cannot be run twice while it is stopped); the one failure is `test_dead_load.test_the_engineers_own_workbooks_still_pass`, which is the Excel defect above and is meant to stay red until the workbook is fixed. The report compiles in German, French and English and at uniform levels 1, 2 and 3, with no warning. The Katalog opens off screen in all three languages, six tabs, and its dependency banner reads correctly in each. `git push` sent v0.54–v0.58, which had never been committed, to GitLab before any of this was touched.

- **v0.58** (2026-09-13) — `#test`, a comparison written by hand that the engine performs (§1, §10bis, §13). The engineer asked for a command that tests a condition and marks it unmistakably — «deux fois soulignée en vert» and a checkmark where it holds, red and a cross where it does not — and asked for it as «une vérification python».

  **There is no such thing, and that shaped the rest.** Typst cannot call Python at compile time: no shell, no FFI, only WASM plugins. So the comparison runs in Typst, which is exact (it compares two floats) and untraced, the standing price of §1 and the one `show-polar-moment` already pays. The traced route is unchanged and still the right one where it is available: a check whose values come from the export belongs in a Python domain, where `status` is data and `show-check` prints it (§14). It could not carry these numbers in any case — the Sherpa capacities of `Sections/005` are read off a manufacturer's table and belong to no workbook.

  **The call.** `#test(left, op, right, unit: "", symbols: none)`. His own sketch, `#test(#fmt(F-Ed) "kN", <=, #fmt(R-d) "kN")`, is not valid Typst on two counts — `#` inside a code-mode argument, and `<=` as a bare value — so the operator is a string and the operands are numbers. Each side may also be a `var` object or a string whose leading number is read out of it, which is his «enlever tout ce qui est textuel afin de garder le numérique» taken literally. **Content is refused**, with a message that says why: `#fmt(x)` returns a `context` block whose `.fields()` is empty, so its output cannot be read back by any code — the number in `$#fmt(F-Ed) "kN"$` is provably unrecoverable, and guessing would be worse than refusing. `symbols:` optionally names the two sides and is the one argument that *does* take content, because a symbol is only drawn and never parsed; a math source string works too, so `get-symbol(var-of(…))` can be handed straight over.

  **What it guarantees, and what it does not.** The mark agrees with the numbers printed beside it, and neither number is retyped. Nothing more: the numbers are still the engineer's, still untraced, and `#test` prints no citation — the prose must carry it, the trade `inline-value` already makes. Its first call site shows why the first half is worth having: `#verdict[$F_"Ed" = 2.037 "kN" lt.eq.slant R_d = 3.294 "kN"$]` restated by hand the `F-Ed` and `R-d` computed six lines above it, and the two would have parted company the first time `a`, `F` or `R-k` changed (§2 rule 1). Two guarantees come free with comparing rather than asserting: units that disagree stop the compile, and a tie is absorbed at a relative `1e-9` — `show-polar-moment`'s own tolerance — while `<` and `>` take that slack off the other side, so `=` and `<` can never both hold of one pair.

  **Everything is appended to `lib/calc-engine.typ` and nothing else moved.** It reuses `quantity-in-formula`, `fmt-decimals`, `number-digits` and the `eval(src, mode: "math")` idiom; `commands.typ` needed no edit, the engine being re-exported with `*` (v0.40). No schema, no record, no data, no Python: **`calc/` was not touched**, and `#test` gets no `call_*` in `build_reference.py` — the `call_*` family turns data the export holds into a pasteable call, and these numbers exist only in the engineer's `#let`s. It needs no glossary key either: it prints digits, a relation glyph and a mark, and **not one word** (§4bis), which is why it reads identically in all three languages.

  ⚠️ **The mark is the heavy pair, U+2714 ✔ and U+2718 ✘, and that is a measurement rather than a preference.** Against Libertinus Serif's own notdef width with `fallback: false`: the heavy pair are real glyphs (6.5 and 6.84 pt against a 5.5 pt notdef), the light ✓ and ✗ are not (5.5 pt, both). The light pair rendered at first only by falling back to Segoe UI Symbol — a sans-serif tick in a serif report, and a blank box on any machine without it — and **Typst 0.14 warns about neither**, so it would have shipped silently. The mark is never bold for the same reason: Libertinus Serif Bold covers neither pair.

  `config.typ`'s `verdict`, which drew the same double rule but took the verdict as an argument, has no caller left; its definition is the engineer's to delete. The two imports that named it explicitly — `commands.typ` and, redundantly, `Sections/005` line 2, which also broke the v0.40 one-import rule — are gone, so that deletion is now a clean single-block delete.

  Verified: the smoke sheet renders both outcomes, all six relations and their three glyph aliases, number/var/string operands, `symbols:` as content and as source, negatives with a real minus, a dimensionless ratio against a bare number, and the three positions — standalone, mid-sentence, and inside `$… \ &= #test(…)$` with the alignment intact and inside a two-column grid without overflowing. `0.1 + 0.2` is green against `0.3` under `<=` and `=`, and red under `<`, `>` and `!=`, in both directions. Eight guards fired on purpose, each with its own message: content, a `#fmt` result, a non-numeric string, an unknown operator, an operator that is not a string, mismatched units, a malformed `symbols:`, and a var whose value is a word. Libertinus Serif's coverage of all four glyphs was measured rather than eyeballed. `build.ps1` builds the report, `-AllLevels` its three uniform variants, and `-Language fr` and `-Language en` both compile — the test line extracted from the German and the French PDFs is byte-identical, as a command that prints no words must be.

- **v0.57** (2026-09-12) — A dimension is drawn as it is being made, and an angle is the one you point at (§3.3octies, §10quater, §13). The three requests the engineer made of v0.55's dimensions, the last of them a defect in their geometry rather than a matter of taste.

  **An angle could only be measured in two of its four sectors**, and that is the one that had to be found before anything else was touched. `member_direction` runs p1 → p2. That is the member's stored direction and **no ray of the corner**: at an L or a T it points away from the corner as often as towards it. `dimension_angle` built its arc between those two stored directions, so it landed in whichever pair of sectors they bounded, and the sign of the radius chose only between that sector and its reflex — two of the four could not be drawn at all. On the engineer's own figure, the beam and the post under it, he wanted the 90° below and to the left and could write nothing but 270°.

  New `angle_rays(source, dim)` chooses the sector instead, from the four the two axes cut the plane into. **`bearing`** — the direction of the third click from the corner, in degrees — names it: the ray of each axis lying on the click's own side bounds the sector the click fell in, and the arc runs the short way between them, at most 180° and any of the four. Checked at 72 bearings, five degrees apart.

  ⚠️ A dimension written **before** `bearing` falls back to the ray running from the corner towards each member's own body — the corner's own geometry, right wherever two members meet end to end — **and its old sign is ignored**. That is the delicate half: the sign never meant a reflex there, it was the only way the engineer had of asking for the other side, the two sectors he wanted being unreachable, so reapplying it would have held his D3 at 270° for ever. It now reads 90°, bisector at 135°, below and to the left, where he pointed, without his touching the file. With a `bearing` a negative radius still writes that sector's reflex, for the figure that wants the 270; a click never writes one, a click always falling inside a sector. The field is optional in `properties` and never in `required`, so a 1.1 file reads as it is, and a `bearing` on a length is refused by name.

  **A witness line no longer runs to what it measures.** «Les traits qui rejoignent les points d'attache ne doivent pas aller jusqu'à ceux-ci»: a line that touches the thing it measures is read as part of the structure. `_witness_gap` draws `DIM_WITNESS_SHARE` of the way — **a quarter**, measured from the dimension line — and leaves the three quarters next to the model empty. The share is the engineer's own, said exactly: «seul 25% du chemin est tracé par la ligne d'attache; les 75% restant jusqu'au point d'attache ne sont pas tracés». The one floor is `DIM_WITNESS_MIN`, for a dimension drawn so close to the model that a quarter of the reach would be nothing at all; the overshoot past the dimension line is unchanged. ⚠️ It was written the other way round first — a quarter left *empty* and three quarters drawn, which is the drafting convention and is not what he asked for: he wants the line to say which point is meant and then get out of the way.

  **And a dimension is drawn while it is being made**, which is what he asked for: «voir à quoi elle ressemble, sa longueur, puis à quoi cela ressemblerait avant le dernier clic». From the first click — with one end down the far end follows the pointer and the length is already read out in the pointer's own line; with both down the line follows the pointer to where it will be drawn; an angle's arc appears as soon as the second member is picked, in the sector the pointer is in, and moves between sectors as the pointer does. `dimension_preview()` and `dimension_readout()` build it from **`model.dimension_line` and `model.dimension_angle`, the very geometry the figure is drawn from**, rather than sketching it by hand here — so what the pointer promises is what the third click delivers. `add_dimension` and `add_angle_dimension` split into `linear_entry`/`angle_entry` and the putting-down, and the preview calls the same constructors. Finally the **member under the pointer** is drawn with the one already picked (`dim_hover`, cleared wherever `dim_members` is), so an angle's two members are chosen by eye.

  Verified: **59 assertions** of its own — the gap at five offsets and at a reach too short to hold it, nothing drawn reaching the measured point, the four sectors all reachable and all different, the short way round at 72 bearings, the reflex still writable, an older dimension repaired rather than frozen, the engineer's own D2 and D3, a bearing refused where it has no meaning, and the preview and the readout driven off screen through both tools from the first click to the third — plus the 124 and 20 of v0.56, all passing. `export_all.py` passes and is idempotent, the report compiles, and the Katalog opens all three models in the three languages. No string changed: the tool's own hint, «l'angle coté est celui du côté où tombe le troisième clic», was already describing what the code now does.

- **v0.56** (2026-09-12) — The articulations say what they mean, and a support's pin is a hinge (§3.3octies, §10quater, §13). Five things the engineer found reading the figures; three of them — the dimensions' extension lines, a live preview between the clicks, and the sector an angle is measured in — belong to v0.55's dimensions and were handed to the session that wrote them. The two that were the drawing's, plus three from the reading before it:

  **The node dot under an Einspannung is gone.** It sat half inside the hatched wall. A node the drawing already has a symbol of takes no dot as well — the rule a release had carried since v0.51, now shared by a wall and by a shared pin.

  **A shear or normal release is centred on its node.** The break was cut from the one member end the source records the freedom against, so it opened entirely on one side and the symbol lay across the other member's line. A release is drawn *on* the node it frees, so the break belongs to the node and not to that bookkeeping: where exactly two member ends meet there, both are drawn back by the same amount; where more than two meet, only the released end is. `_release_cuts` replaces the per-member reading, and `_runs` drops a stretch of no length, which SVG's round cap would otherwise draw as a dot — putting back, at the node, the very mark the break was opened to clear.

  **And the two releases want opposite things of that break.** A *shear* release's halves slide across each other: its two bars stand one each side of the node, the members come up to them and stop clear. A *normal* release's halves slide **along** each other: its two lines are a **sleeve**, and the two ends have to be seen **inside** it (`RELEASE_SLIDE`), leaving a break about as wide as a bar is thick. The engineer's words: «les deux barres doivent s'insérer partiellement entre les deux petits traits horizontaux, sinon l'articulation ne fait pas sens». A sleeve around nothing says nothing — which is what the centring alone had made it. The break is measured from the **drawn** ends, half a stroke inside the nominal ones, so it holds its width at any setting of the `line` and `point` sliders; `RELEASE_SLEEVE` widens the two lines off the axis, without which the sleeve and the bar run together into one band at the size the report prints.

  **A Festlager and a Loslager carry the hinge circle**, and where it sits is the source's to say (`SUPPORT_HINGES`): `shared`, the pin the bar turns about, drawn on the node — the default, so a file without the field reads as it is; or `through`, a bar running over the support, the pin set back along the support's own axis until the circle just touches the bar, the support hanging under it, apex against the circle. An Einspannung has no pin and a `hinge` on one is refused by name. Source schema 1.1 (`properties`, never `required`), a row in the support's panel and a column in the points' list, in the three languages; `_hinge` is the one circle all three draw — a moment release, a support's pin, a spring's centre — so they cannot come to differ.

  **A rotational spring's centre is that circle too**, its coil opening from the circle's own edge: «le point central doit être blanc comme une articulation de moment, sinon cela ne fait pas sens statiquement». It is right — a rotational spring frees the moment like any hinge. ⚠️ The coil turns a **quarter past a whole turn** (`SPRING_TURNS` 1.25), and that is not decoration: it starts along the member's own axis, so a whole or a half turn ends it back on that axis, where the member's line — thicker than the coil — swallows the free end, and two rings around a hinge are what is left. Four variants were drawn and read before choosing; 1.5 and 2 turns both came out as targets. `_release_extent` then sizes what is measured against the symbol — the frame, the halo, and how far its own `k = …` label stands off — since a coil reaches further than anything else drawn at a node.

  **A plain node is the editor's alone.** `render(..., nodes: True)`, passed by the Katalog and by its tool icons and by nothing else: a point carrying neither a support nor a release is not an articulation, and a dot on it in the report would claim it is one. In the window it says where one may click.

  Verified: **124 assertions on the drawing and the source** and **20 on the tab**, the latter driven off screen in the real window — among them the break centred and the same whichever half the source records it against, the ends inside the sleeve and not touching, the break holding its width at five slider settings and cleared by a cable's own thinner pen, the shear release unchanged, the coil starting at the circle's edge and its free end clear of the member's axis, the frame, halo and label following the coil, a `hinge` refused on an Einspannung and dropped when a support becomes one, the cell closed where it has no meaning, and the draft surviving the three languages. `export_all.py` passes and is byte-identical run twice; the report compiles and page 5.1 reads correctly; QtSvg reads all three models and all eighteen tool icons. The figures were read at 4× on the engineer's own system before and after each change — which is the real test here, and what caught the ringed spring.

- **v0.55** (2026-09-12) — A static model can be dimensioned: four tools in the Typst-Katalog (§3.3octies, §10bis, §10ter, §10quater, §11, §13, §14). The engineer asked for tools to write a dimension on the drawing — the length of a bar or a cable, and the angle between two of them — in **three clicks**: «clique 1: début de la cotation, clique 2: fin de la cotation, clique 3: distance perpendiculaire aux 3 points à laquelle dessiner la côte», with three tools, horizontal, vertical and parallel to the element. Asked whether the angle should be a mode of those three or a tool of its own, they chose **a fourth tool**.

  **The data.** A source carries a seventh collection, `dimensions`, one entry per dimension: its `id` (prefix `D`), its `kind` — `horizontal`, `vertical`, `parallel` or `angle` — its two ends, its `offset` and an optional `label`. One collection with a discriminator rather than two, for the reason §13 now states. An end is an **anchor**: a point of the model, which the dimension then follows as that point is moved, or a place of its own; for an angle, a member. The `offset` is one signed number in metres, measured from the **middle of the two ends** at right angles to the dimension line — so the line follows the model rather than staying where the third click fell — and for an angle it is the radius of the arc, a negative one drawing the other arc of the pair, which is how 90° and 270° are told apart. Sources are `schema_version` **1.1**; a 1.0 file, carrying none, is read exactly as it is. The export is **1.1** as well, which also repairs v0.52's change of its shape (`figure` → `figures`) made without bumping the version.

  **The drawing.** `statics_svg` places each dimension in `layout()` and pushes its boxes into the frame's accumulator, so a dimension set well clear of the model widens the figure rather than being cut off. A length is drawn as two witness lines, the dimension line with a slim head at either end, and the value over it, turned with its own line; an angle as its arc sampled into a polyline — SVG Tiny has no arc command — with a head at either end and a thin tie from the corner to each. Both stand in **their own marked region**, `<!-- dims -->…<!-- /dims -->`, between the structure and the loads, and their values in a second one: `show-static-model(…, dimensions: false)` takes them alone, `loads: false` leaves them standing, and `highlight` may name one.

  **The window.** A **seventh box** in the tool bar, *Cotations / Bemassung / Dimensions*, whose four icons are drawn by the figure's own code; the first tools here that take three clicks, the angle's two first naming members, with a refusal said in words for two ends in the same place, the same member twice, and two members that do not cross. A **fifth list** under the drawing: id, type, de, à, décalage, valeur (read only — it is the model's answer) and texte. And the dimension's own panel, with its measured value, its offset and that text. Thirty words in the three languages.

  **Elsewhere:** `schemas/statics-source.schema.json` and `schemas/statics.schema.json` gained their blocks, `validation.py` its `STATIC_DIMENSION_KINDS` and its rules, `lib/calc-engine.typ` the `dimensions:` argument and a dimension's halo, and `build_reference.py` two columns on its `Statics` sheet.

  Verified:
  - **328 checks** pass across the four suites — 111 on the model, 81 on the drawing, 67 on the dimensions' own drawing, 38 on the export, 142 on the window (the last three including 18 guards fired on purpose, each naming what to fix: an unknown kind, an end at an unknown point, an angle on an unknown member, an angle of a member with itself, an angle between parallels, a length naming a member, an end that is neither a point nor a place, a non-numeric offset, two ends in the same place, an id already taken, a label that is not text, and six on the exported record).
  - A 1.0 source still reads and exports, with an empty list; a 1.1 source round-trips unchanged; a model ten times the size draws the same figure to the byte once its values are held still, and it is the values that change with it.
  - `python calc/export_all.py` passes and is byte-identical run twice; `.\build.ps1 -AllLevels` builds the report and its three variants.
  - The four kinds were drawn on a portal frame and rendered by Typst itself: with dimensions and loads, with `loads: false`, with `dimensions: false`, with neither, and with a halo on a length and on an angle — six figures, each correct.
  - The three clicks were driven off screen in the real window: the first two draw nothing, the third writes the dimension; a click on a point anchors to it and a click on nothing keeps its place; moving that point changes the value; the angle's third click chooses which of the two angles is meant; Escape gives a half-drawn one up; and a point removed takes with it the dimensions that named it, through the members too.
  - The tool bar was grabbed with Windows' own fonts in all three languages: **seven boxes**, nothing cut short, one button height.

- **v0.54** (2026-09-12) — The document's language and the engine's language are two settings, and three oversights repaired (§4bis, §10bis, §13, §14). The engineer used v0.52 and reported four things, warning that they might need «une restructuration en profondeur». They needed one change of principle and three repairs.

  **The principle.** v0.52 made *one* command move everything displayed, and that was too wide. `#set-report-language("fr")` also translated the section headings and the document's title — words the engineer had typed himself — and, because `config.typ` pulled `text(lang:)` out of the language state, it also flipped Typst's own localisation: «Abbildung» became «Fig.», the outline's title changed and the hyphenation went with it. The fix is a **separation**, not an addition (§13): the **document's** language is `doc-lang`/`doc-region`, declared by hand at the top of `config.typ`, and governs what Typst writes of its own accord; the **engine's** language stays `#set-report-language` in the entry file and governs only what the glossary translates; and what the engineer writes — headings, title, prose, a `title:` or a `caption:` — is touched by neither. A French report edits two files, which is the point: each setting answers a different question.

  **What changed for it.** `config.typ` lost its `lib/i18n.typ` import and its `context { set text(lang: report-language.final()); doc }`, so the file is again what its own header promises — plain `set`/`show` rules, no state — with `doc-lang`/`doc-region` at the top and applied in `conf`. The 21 `#t("section.…")` calls of `Sections/001`–`007` and the `#t("report.title")` of the title page went back to the German words they had (Einleitung, Grundlagen, Positionsplan, Pläne, Baustoffe, Verbindungsmittel, Einwirkungen, Eigengewicht, Nutzlast, Schneelast, Windlast, Tragwerksanalyse, Statische Systeme, Nachweise, Literatur, Statischer Bericht) — taken from the glossary's own `de` values, so the restoration is exact — and the **sixteen keys were removed** from `calc/i18n/strings.json`, which now holds 48 `engine` keys, 18 `templates` and 145 `terms`. `lib/i18n.typ`'s two doc comments that used `section.actions` as their example were rewritten: `t` is for a caller in markup, never for a heading.

  **The terrain categories, read from the norm.** All four stayed German in a French report — «IV grossflächige Stadtgebiete» — because they are workbook values, and v0.52's rule left workbook values alone. But SIA 261 **names** them: `000-ExternalFiles/000-Normes/SIA261-2020-FR.pdf` p. 31 and `SIA261-2020-DE.pdf` p. 30 set Tableau/Tabelle 4 out side by side, and the two editions give *Seeufer* → *rive lacustre*, *grosse Ebene* → *grande plaine*, *Ortschaften, freies Feld* → *localité, milieu rural*, *grossflächige Stadtgebiete* → *zone urbaine étendue*. Four `terms` entries, quoted (§2 rule 13). That sharpens the rule rather than bending it: **a value the norm names is the norm's word even when it arrives from a cell**, and a value only the workbook names («Flachdach», «Ja») stays the engineer's — the glossary decides, as always. The English is a translation and the file says so, there being no English edition of SIA 261 and no EN 1991-1-4 in the library; annex H's Tabelle 80 covers chapter 1's terms only and was checked. One print site was missing besides: `var-row` printed a choice's value and its text **raw**, so the four footnote lines stayed German even once the value on the line above was translated. Both now go through the glossary.

  **A sentence in the data may carry math.** `cred` had to read `c_red`, and a note was a plain string — `wind.py` said so in a comment: «an underscore in it would be printed as one». **`render-prose`** lifts that: a printed sentence is split on `$` and what stands between a pair is evaluated as Typst math, exactly as a `symbol` or a `formula` already is (§3.1, §6bis). It is applied at the six sites that print a sentence from the data — a table's `note`, a var's `description` and each of its `choices` (the value **and** the text beside it), a step's `description`, and a live load's footnote. Then the symbols were written as math in the German source and in all three glossary variants, the German being the key: `$c_f$` and `$c_"red"$` in the global wind note, `$c_"pe"$` and `$c_"pi"$` in the local one, `$z_g$` and `$alpha_r$` in the terrain choices. `b·h`, `d·h` and `d·b` stay words — they are the table's own column headings, and changing them on one side only would show. Splitting on the dollar interprets nothing else, and an **unpaired** dollar leaves the sentence exactly as it is, so a description reading «5$ pro Stück» is safe; no string in `data/` or in the glossary contained a `$` before this.

  **The German left in a legend.** In the French *Forces globales du vent* note, one fragment was still German: «Tabelle «Globale Kraftbeiwerte»», the name of a block in the engineer's workbook. Translated without keeping the original, as he chose — fr *tableau des coefficients de force globaux*, en *table of the global force coefficients* — and the same for `glm. (+)`/`glm. (-)` in the local note, which become *uniforme (+) / (-)* and *uniform (+) / (-)*, matching the column the table already headed *uniforme*. The sheet's name (`WL-Velo`) stays: it is an identifier. Only the fr/en variants changed; the German keeps the name the workbook carries.

  No schema, no record shape and no figure changed.

  Verified:
  - `python calc/export_all.py` passes and is idempotent (every file in `data/` byte-identical on a second run). Compared field by field with the previous export, `data/calculations.json` differs in **exactly 24 leaves**: the 2 notes and 4 `choices.desc` of each of the 4 WL sheets, and nothing else.
  - **The German report is unchanged where it had to be**: 12 pages before and after, and the only text that differs is on pages 8 and 9, where `cf`, `cred`, `cpe`, `cpi`, `zg` and `αr` are now real math. The headings, «Abbildung», «Inhaltsverzeichnis» and the hyphenation are identical — which is the core of the test.
  - **The French report** (`build.ps1 -Language fr`): the four categories in French on the value line *and* in all four footnote lines, both wind notes without a word of German, `c_red` as math — while «Statischer Bericht», every heading, «Abbildung» and «Inhaltsverzeichnis» stayed German. Then `doc-lang = "fr"` in `config.typ`: «Fig. 2. –», «Table des matières» and French hyphenation followed, and the headings and title stayed German. Both halves of the separation, each doing only its own half.
  - v0.52's leftover-German scan relaunched over the fr and en PDFs, on word boundaries and with context, against all 209 German strings the glossary carries: **no leak**. Every hit is the architect's own plan text (`Betondecke`, `Plan-Nr.`, `OK Brüstung`), the engineer's prose, or a workbook value the rule leaves German (`Stütze`, `EL+AL-Dach mit Wasser`) — printed, in the same line, beside engine words that did translate (*Classe d'humidité*).
  - Guards, each fired on purpose: an unpaired `$` leaves its sentence intact (as do three unpaired), a paired one becomes math, several pairs in one sentence all do, a sentence without one passes through, and non-string content passes through; a section still calling a removed key fails the compile with «t-of: no string "section.actions" in the glossary -- see calc/i18n/strings.json»; and none of the sixteen keys appears anywhere in the tree.
  - **1226 calls** — every one the Katalog offers for the current data — compile together in each of the three languages. `build.ps1 -AllLevels` builds the report and its three variants; `make`, `make report REPORT_LANG=fr` and a `make` run with `LANG=en_US.UTF-8` in the environment all behave (the last ignores it, §12). The Katalog starts off screen in French, German and English, six tabs each, and lists the loads.

- **v0.53** (2026-09-12) — The reference library, `000-ExternalFiles/` (§2, §3.3quinquies, §3.3septies, §3.3sexies, §4bis, §11, §11bis, §14). The engineer put the documents this project is calculated against inside the project — the norms, the literature, the course documents of eleven calculation topics, and a set of third-party tools, «souvent html», «déjà codés mais non vérifiés» — and asked that this document record that they exist and that **the norms are the single source of truth for the calculations**.

  **Documentation only.** Not one line of `calc/`, of `lib/`, of `Sections/` or of a schema changed, nothing was re-exported and no report was rebuilt: the folder is read by the engineer and by the assistant and by nothing else, and the one place the engine meets it is where a norm's numbers are transcribed by hand into `calc/norms/*.json` (§11).
